\frac{5m}{4} &= -\frac{3}{4} \\
m &= -\frac{3}{5}
\end{align*}
\]

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Ordering Vertices**: Arrange the points in a consistent clockwise or counter-clockwise order to ensure the polygon doesn't intersect itself.
2. **Shoelace Formula**:
   - List the coordinates in order (e.g., A, B, C, D) and repeat the first point at the end.
   - Multiply each x-coordinate by the next y-coordinate and sum them up for S1.
   - Multiply each y-coordinate by the next x-coordinate and sum them up for S2.
3. **Calculate Area**: Take half of the absolute difference between S1 and S2.

For points A(-6,7), B(2,9), C(7,-3), D(-2,0):

- Ordered as A→B→C→D→A
- Compute:
  - S1 = (-6×9) + (2×-3) + (7×0) + (-2×7) = -54 -6 +0 -14 = -74
  - S2 = (7×2) + (9×7) + (-3×-2) + (0×-6) = 14 +63 +6 +0 =83
- Area = |S1 - S2| /2 = |-74 -83| /2 = 157/2 =78.5

(5/8)x - y - 17/4 = 0
Multiply all terms by 8 to eliminate denominators:
5x - 8y - 34 = 0

So the equation is 5x - 8y - 34 = 0.

Now, the formula for the distance from a point (x0, y0) to the line Ax + By + C = 0 is:

Distance = |Ax0 + By0 + C| / sqrt(A^2 + B^2)

Here, A =5, B= -8, C= -34.

Point P is (2, -2), so x0=2, y0=-2.

Numerator: |5*2 + (-8)*(-2) + (-34)| = |10 + 16 -34| = |-8| = 8

Denominator: sqrt(5^2 + (-8)^2) = sqrt(25 +64)=sqrt(89)

So distance is 8 / sqrt(89). To rationalize the denominator, multiply numerator and denominator by sqrt(89):

Distance = (8*sqrt(89))/89

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to determine if three points form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem. The points given are (4,4), (3,5), and (-1,-1). Hmm, let's see how to approach this.

First, I remember that in coordinate geometry, we can check for right angles by looking at the slopes of two lines meeting at a point. If two sides are perpendicular, their slopes should multiply to -1 because perpendicular lines have slopes that are negative reciprocals of each other.

So, let's label the points as A(4,4), B(3,5), and C(-1,-1). Now, I need to find the slopes between these points. 

Let me calculate the slope between A and B first. The formula for slope is (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). So between A and B: 
Slope AB = (5-4)/(3-4) = 1/(-1) = -1.

Next, I'll find the slope between B and C:
Slope BC = (-1 -5)/(-1 -3) = (-6)/(-4) = 3/2.

Then, the slope between A and C would be useful too. Let me compute that:
Slope AC = (-1 -4)/(-1 -4) = (-5)/(-5) = 1.

Wait a second, I need to check if any two of these slopes multiply to -1 because that indicates perpendicularity. So let's see:

- Slope AB is -1 and slope BC is 3/2. Multiplying them: (-1)*(3/2) = -3/2 ≠ -1.
- Slope BC is 3/2, slope AC is 1. Multiplying these gives (3/2)*1 = 3/2 ≠ -1.
- Slope AB is -1 and slope AC is 1. Multiplying: (-1)*(1) = -1.

Ah! There we go. The product of slopes AB and AC is -1, which means they are perpendicular to each other. So the triangle formed by points A, B, and C has a right angle at point A because the lines AB and AC meet at A with slopes that are negative reciprocals.

Slope AB: (5-4)/(3-4) = 1/(-1) = -1 ✔️

Slope AC: (-1 -4)/(-1 -4) = (-5)/(-5) = 1 ✔️

Multiplying slopes of AB and AC: -1 * 1 = -1 ✔️

Yes, that seems correct. So the triangle is right-angled at A.

Dot product: (1)(-sqrt(3)) + (sqrt(3))(1) = -sqrt(3) + sqrt(3) = 0

Therefore theta is arccos(0) = 90°, confirming that they are perpendicular.

Thus the angle between line and its normal is 90 degrees, which we already knew because by definition. So if problem was "What's the angle between a line and its normal?" then answer is always 90°, so \boxed{90^\circ} as above.

But in original thought process, he said: "Let me think... What's the angle between line and its normal? Let me think..." So maybe it was more elaborate—perhaps trying to find some other relation—but since question seems standard, I believe answer is 90°.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{90^\circ}
</think>

To determine the angle between a line and its normal, we start by noting that for any straight line, the slope of the line (let's denote it as \( m \)) and the slope of its normal are negative reciprocals. This means if the slope of the line is \( m \), the slope of the normal is \( -\frac{1}{m} \).

Given that the normal makes a 60° angle with the positive y-axis, we can find the direction of the normal vector. The angle between the normal and the positive x-axis would be 150° (since 90° + 60° = 150°). The slope of this normal is \( \tan(150°) \), which is \( -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3} \).

The line itself, being perpendicular to the normal, will have a slope that is the negative reciprocal of \( -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3} \). This gives us a slope of \( \sqrt{3} \) for the line. The angle this line makes with the positive x-axis is 60° (since \( \tan^{-1}(\sqrt{3}) = 60° \)).

However, regardless of the specific angles and slopes, the key point is that a line and its normal are always perpendicular to each other by definition. Therefore, the angle between the line and its normal is always 90°.

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{90^\circ}
\]

cos(theta) = (v ⋅ w)/(|v||w|)

Where v=(1,-1), w=(0,1):

Dot product: (1)(0) + (-1)(1)= -1

|v|=sqrt(1+1)=√2
|w|=1

The angle formed by a line with a slope of -1 (which means it descends at a rate where for every unit moved to the right, y decreases by one) relative to the Y-axis can be determined using vector analysis. The direction vector of this line is (1, -1), and the Y-axis can be represented by the vector (0, 1). Calculating the angle between these vectors yields:

cos(theta) = |(1*0 + (-1)*1)| / (√2 * 1)  
cos(theta) = |-1| / √2 ≈ 0.7071

This results in theta being 45 degrees.

The problem involves finding the equations of two lines with a specific angular relationship, given that one line is twice as steep as the other and their acute angle satisfies $\tan \theta = \frac{1}{3}$. 

To solve this:

1. Let $m$ be the slope of one line; then the slope of the other line is $2m$.
2. The tangent of the angle θ between two lines with slopes $m_1$ and $m_2$ is given by:
   \[
   \tan \theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
   \]
3. Substituting $m_2 = 2m_1 = 2m$, we get:
   \[
   \tan \theta = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + (m)(2m)} \right| = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
   \]
4. Setting this equal to $\frac{1}{3}$ and solving for $m$ gives:
   \[
   \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
   \]
5. Solving the resulting quadratic equations leads to four possible values of $m$: $-1$, $-\frac{1}{2}$, $\frac{1}{2}$, and $1$.

**Answer:**
The slopes are $\boxed{-\dfrac{1}{2}}$, $\boxed{-1}$, $\boxed{\dfrac{1}{2}}$, and $\boxed{1}$.

Sure! Let's find the equation of the line that passes through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7).

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope
The slope (\(m\)) of a line passing through two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

Substituting the given points (3, 1) and (5, 7):
\[
m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation
The point-slope form of a line is:
\[ y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \]

Using one of the points, say (3, 1):
\[
y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
\]

Simplify the equation to get it in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
\[
y - 1 = 3x - 9 \\
y = 3x - 8
\]

### Final Answer:
The equation of the line is:
\[ \boxed{y = 3x - 8} \]

First, I'll assign the points:
P1 = (3, 0)

To determine if the three points \( (3, 0) \), \( (-2, -2) \), and \( (8, 2) \) are collinear, we can use the concept of slopes.

1. **Calculate the slope between the first two points:**
   \[
   m_1 = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

2. **Calculate the slope between the last two points:**
   \[
   m_2 = \frac{y_3 - y_2}{x_3 - x_2} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{8 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{10} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

Since both slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) are equal, the three points lie on the same straight line.

To determine the volume \( V \) when the pressure \( P \) is 117 kPa, we can use the linear relationship between \( P \) and \( V \). Given two points:

- When \( P = 114 \) kPa, \( V = 1080 \) liters.
- When \( P = 132 \) kPa, \( V = 1160 \) liters.

First, calculate the slope \( m \) of the line:

\[
m = \frac{\Delta V}{\Delta P} = \frac{1160 - 1080}{132 - 114} = \frac{80}{18} = \frac{40}{9}
\]

Using the point-slope form with \( (P_1, V_1) = (114, 1080) \):

\[
V - 1080 = \frac{40}{9}(P - 114)
\]

Substitute \( P = 117 \) kPa:

\[
V = 1080 + \frac{40}{9}(117 - 114) = 1080 + \frac{40}{9} \times 3 = 1080 + \frac{120}{9} \approx 1080 + 13.333 = 1093.33
\]

**Answer:** The volume is approximately \boxed{1093.33} liters when the pressure is 117 kPa.

Given the problem of finding the vector equation of a line with x-intercept \(a\) and y-intercept \(b\) such that \(a + b = 13\) and the line passes through the point \((4, 4)\), we start by analyzing the conditions provided.

First, we know that the equation of a line in intercept form is:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]
Since the line passes through the point \((4, 4)\), substituting \(x = 4\) and \(y = 4\) into the equation gives:
\[
\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{b} = 1
\]
We also know from the problem statement that:
\[
a + b = 13
\]

We now have two equations:
1. \(\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{b} = 1\)
2. \(a + b = 13\)

To solve these, we substitute \(b = 13 - a\) into the first equation:
\[
\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{13 - a} = 1
\]

Multiplying through by \(a(13 - a)\) to clear the denominators, we get:
\[
4(13 - a) + 4a = a(13 - a)
\]
Simplifying this equation:
\[
52 - 4a + 4a = 13a - a^2
\]
This reduces to:
\[
52 = 13a - a^2
\]
Rearranging terms, we obtain the quadratic equation:
\[
a^2 - 13a + 52 = 0
\]

Calculating the discriminant of this quadratic equation:
\[
D = (-13)^2 - 4 \cdot 1 \cdot 52 = 169 - 208 = -39
\]
Since the discriminant is negative, the quadratic equation has no real solutions. This implies that there are no real values of \(a\) and \(b\) that satisfy both given conditions.

Therefore, we conclude that there is no real vector equation for such a line because the system of equations derived from the given conditions has no real solutions.

\[
\boxed{\text{No such vector equation exists}}
\]

The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x - 6y -28 = 0 is calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line:

- **Given values**: A=5, B=-6, C=-28; Point (5, -7)

The given lines are parallel because they have identical coefficients for x and y, ensuring their slopes are equal. To find the distance between them, we use the formula:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|C_2 - C_1|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Here:
- \( A = 3 \)
- \( B = -4 \)
- \( C_1 = 12 \) (from the first line)
- \( C_2 = 7 \) (from the second line)

Plugging in these values:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|7 - 12|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{9 + 16}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

The distance between the two parallel lines is 1 unit.

   \[ m_{BC} = \frac{4 - 1}{3 - 6} = \frac{3}{-3} = -1 \]

To determine the value of \( a \) such that the given lines are parallel, we need to compare their slopes. 

1. The slope of the line \( 7x - 9y = k \) (given line) is calculated as follows:
   \[
   y = \frac{7}{9}x + c
   \]
   Here, the slope is \( \frac{7}{9} \).

2. For the line EH with equation involving parameter \( a \), we need to find its slope. The slope is given by \( \frac{2}{a - 6} \).

3. To make the lines parallel, their slopes must be equal:
   \[
   \frac{7}{9} = \frac{2}{a - 6}
   \]

4. Solving for \( a \):
   \[
   7(a - 6) = 18
   \]
   \[
   7a - 42 = 18
   \]
   \[
   7a = 60
   \]
   \[
   a = \frac{60}{7}
   \]

Thus, the value of \( a \) is \(\boxed{\dfrac{60}{7}}\).

SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/S

***********************<think>
Okay, so I have this problem to solve. The equation of the line given is X - 7y + 5 = 0. I need to find the equation of a line that's perpendicular to this one and passes through the x-intercept at (5,0). Hmm, let me break this down step by step.

First off, I remember that two lines are perpendicular if the product of their slopes is -1. So I should probably start by finding the slope of the given line. To do that, it's easier to rewrite the equation in slope-intercept form, which is y = mx + b, where m is the slope.

Starting with X - 7y + 5 = 0, let me rearrange this:
-7y = -X - 5
Divide both sides by -7 to solve for y:
y = (1/7)X + (5/7)
So the slope (m) of the given line is 1/7.

Since the perpendicular line's slope should be the negative reciprocal, that means m_perpendicular = -7. Because multiplying 1/7 by -7 gives -1, which satisfies the condition for perpendicularity.

Now I know the slope of the new line is -7 and it passes through (5,0). Using the point-slope form of a line equation, which is y - y1 = m(x - x1), where (x1,y1) is a point on the line.

Plugging in the values:
y - 0 = -7(x - 5)
Simplifying that:
y = -7x + 35

Wait, let me double-check. If I plug in x=5 into this equation, y should be 0:
y = -7*5 + 35
y = -35 + 35
y = 0. Yep, that's correct.

So the equation of the perpendicular line is y = -7x + 35. To write it in standard form (Ax + By + C = 0), I can rearrange it:
7x + y - 35 = 0

To find the equation of the line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) that passes through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Slope**:
   
   The given line can be rewritten in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)) as:
   \[
   -6y = -5x - 4 \\
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]
   So, the slope \(m\) is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

2. **Use Point-Slope Form**:

   The point-slope form of a line is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   Substituting the point \((-4, 5)\) and slope \(m = \frac{5}{6}\):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]

3. **Simplify the Equation**:

   Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate fractions:
   \[
   6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4) \\
   6y - 30 = 5x + 20
   \]
   
   Rearrange terms to standard form (\(Ax + By + C = 0\)):
   \[
   5x - 6y + 50 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer**
\[
\boxed{5x - 6y + 50 = 0}
\]

To solve the given system of equations:

1) \(24x + 13y = 760\)

2) \(-5x + y = \frac{9}{8}\)

First, solve equation 2 for \(y\):

\[
-5x + y = \frac{9}{8} \implies y = 5x + \frac{9}{8}
\]

Substitute this expression for \(y\) into equation 1:

\[
24x + 13(5x + \frac{9}{8}) = 760
\]

Expand and simplify:

\[
24x + 65x + \frac{117}{8} = 760
\]
\[
89x + \frac{117}{8} = 760
\]

Subtract \(\frac{117}{8}\) from both sides:

\[
89x = 760 - \frac{117}{8}
\]
\[
89x = \frac{6080}{8} - \frac{117}{8}
\]
\[
89x = \frac{5963}{8}
\]

Solve for \(x\):

\[
x = \frac{5963}{8 \times 89} = \frac{5963}{712}
\]
\[
x = \frac{67}{8}
\]

Substitute \(x = \frac{67}{8}\) back into the expression for \(y\):

\[
y = 5 \left(\frac{67}{8}\right) + \frac{9}{8} = \frac{335}{8} + \frac{9}{8} = \frac{344}{8} = 43
\]

Thus, the solution to the system of equations is:

\[
\boxed{\left( \frac{67}{8}, 43 \right)}
\]

Okay, let me try to figure out how to solve this problem. So, I need to find the distance from the point (1, 2) to the line given by the equation 4x + 7y + 5 = 0. Hmm, I remember there's a formula for calculating the distance from a point to a line in coordinate geometry.

The general form of a line is Ax + By + C = 0, and the formula for the distance (d) from a point (x₀, y₀) to this line is:

\[ d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Alright, so in this case, A is 4, B is 7, and C is 5. The point (x₀, y₀) is (1, 2).

Let me plug these values into the formula:

\[ d = \frac{|4*1 + 7*2 + 5|}{\sqrt{4^2 + 7^2}} \]

First, calculate the numerator:

4*1 = 4

7*2 = 14

Adding them together with C: 4 + 14 + 5 = 23. Since it's absolute value, |23| is still 23.

Now, for the denominator:

4 squared is 16, and 7 squared is 49. Adding these gives 16 + 49 = 65. The square root of 65 is √65.

So putting it all together:

\[ d = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}} \]

To rationalize the denominator, I can multiply numerator and denominator by √65:

\[ d = \frac{23 \sqrt{65}}{65} \]

Therefore, the distance from the point (1, 2) to the line 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 is \( \frac{23\sqrt{65}}{65} \).

But wait, I think it's also common to leave it as \( \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}} \) since sometimes leaving a square root in the denominator is acceptable depending on the context. So both forms are correct; it's just a matter of preference or what is required.

So, after considering both possibilities, I'll present both forms to ensure clarity.
</think>

The distance from the point (1, 2) to the line 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 can be calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line in coordinate geometry. 

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Identify the coefficients**: From the equation 4x + 7y + 5 = 0, we have A = 4, B = 7, and C = 5.
2. **Apply the distance formula**:
   \[ d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]
3. **Plug in the values**:
   - Numerator: \( |4*1 + 7*2 + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = |23| = 23 \)
   - Denominator: \( \sqrt{4^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{16 + 49} = \sqrt{65} \)
4. **Calculate the distance**:
   \[ d = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}} \]
   
   Rationalizing the denominator gives:
   \[ d = \frac{23 \sqrt{65}}{65} \]

Both forms are correct and represent the same distance.

To find the **lope** (slope) of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\), we'll use the slope formula:

From what I remember, the shoelace formula involves multiplying the x-coordinate of each vertex by the y-coordinate of the next vertex and summing all those products. Then you do the same for the y-coordinates multiplied by the x-coordinates of the next vertices. Finally, subtract these two sums and take half the absolute value to get the area.

Point B: (2, 9)
Point C: (7, -3)

Let me set up two sums:

Sum1 = (x_A * y_B) + (x_B * y_C) + (x_C * y_D) + (x_D * y_A)
Sum2 = (y_A * x_B) + (y_B * x_C) + (y_C * x_D) + (y_D * x_A)

y_D * x_A = 0 * (-6) = 0

Taking the absolute value gives | -157 | = 157.

Finally, half of this is 157 / 2 = 78.5.

So, the area should be 78.5 square units.

The area of the quadrilateral with vertices (-6,7), (2,9), (7,-3), and (-2,0) is calculated using the shoelace formula. Applying the coordinates in order:

Sum1 = (-6*9) + (2*-3) + (7*0) + (-2*7) = -54 -6 + 0 -14 = -74  
Sum2 = (7*2) + (9*7) + (-3*-2) + (0*-6) = 14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83  

Area = (|Sum1 - Sum2|)/2 = |(-74) - 83| / 2 = |-157| / 2 = 78.5.

To solve problems involving angles relative to coordinate axes, particularly when lines are inclined at specific angles like 60 degrees from the Y-axis anti-clockwise, follow these structured steps:

1. **Identify Given Information**: Recognize whether you're dealing with a line's slope, distance from origin, or displacement components.

2. **Determine Line Equation**:
   - If given an angle θ (e.g., 60 degrees) relative to the Y-axis, determine the corresponding slope using trigonometric identities.
   - For example, if a line makes 60 degrees with the Y-axis anti-clockwise, its slope m is tan(150°), which equals -√3/3.

3. **Apply Distance Formula**:
   - Use the distance formula from a point to a line: \( D = \frac{|ax_0 + by_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}} \).
   - For instance, if you know the equation of the line and a point's coordinates, plug them into this formula.

4. **Utilize Trigonometric Relationships**:
   - Break down displacements or vectors into components using cosine for adjacent sides (e.g., x-component) and sine for opposite sides (e.g., y-component).
   - For a distance d at 60 degrees from the Y-axis, calculate \( x = d \cdot \cos(150^\circ) \) and \( y = d \cdot \sin(150^\circ) \).

5. **Consider Rotation Matrices**:
   - If dealing with vectors or points after rotation by θ (e.g., 60 degrees anti-clockwise), apply the rotation matrix:
     \[
     R(\theta) = 
     \begin{bmatrix}
     \cos\theta & -\sin\theta \\
     \sin\theta & \cos\theta
     \end{bmatrix}
     \]
   - Use this to rotate vectors or coordinates accordingly.

By following these steps, you can systematically approach and solve problems involving angles relative to coordinate axes. For a specific problem like finding the projection of a vector at 60 degrees from Y-axis, use trigonometric breakdowns with sine and cosine values for accurate results.

The problem requires finding the slopes of two lines such that one slope is twice the other and the tangent of the angle between them is 1/3. 

By solving the equation derived from the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines, we find four possible pairs of slopes:

- (0.5, 1)
- (1, 2)
- (-0.5, -1)
- (-1, -2)

Each pair consists of a slope and twice that slope, both positive and negative values are considered.

**Answer:**
The possible slopes are $\boxed{\frac{1}{2}}$ and $\boxed{1}$; $\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}}$ and $\boxed{-1}$.

   - This means the slope \(m\) of the line is \(\frac{1}{4}\).

2. Considering the intercept:
   - The term "�য়াই-ইন্টারসেপ্ট" translates to y-intercept in English.
   - Assuming the y-intercept (the value where the line crosses the y-axis) is \(-\frac{5}{4}\).

3. Forming the equation of the line:
   - Using the slope-intercept form \(y = mx + b\), where \(m\) is the slope and \(b\) is the y-intercept.
   - Substituting \(m = \frac{1}{4}\) and \(b = -\frac{5}{4}\).

Okay, let's break this down step by step.

1. **Understand What’s Given:**
   - We know that tan θ = 1/6.
   - The x-intercept is at (6, 0).

2. **Find the Slope (m):**
   - Since tan θ gives us the slope of the line in a right triangle where opposite side over adjacent side represents rise over run, so m = 1/6.

3. **Use Point-Slope Form:**
   - The point-slope form is y - y₁ = m(x - x₁).
   - Plugging in the point (6, 0), we get:
     y - 0 = (1/6)(x - 6).

4. **Simplify the Equation:**
   - Distribute the slope on the right side.
   - So, y = (1/6)x - (1/6)*6 → y = (1/6)x - 1.

5. **Final Equation in Slope-Intercept Form:**
   - The equation of the line is y = (1/6)x - 1.

**Answer:**  
The equation of the line is \boxed{y = \frac{1}{6}x - 1}.

First, I need to determine the equation of a line that has x-intercept -1 and y-intercept 4. 

The intercept form of a linear equation is \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1\), where \(a\) is the x-intercept and \(b\) is the y-intercept.

Substituting the given intercepts into the formula, we get:
\[
\frac{x}{-1} + \frac{y}{4} = 1
\]

To simplify this equation to slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)), I'll solve for \(y\):
\[
-\frac{x}{1} + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \\
\frac{y}{4} = x + 1 \\
y = 4x + 4
\]

Thus, the equation of the line in slope-intercept form is:
\[
y = 4x + 4
\]

Okay, I need to determine if three points are collinear. The points given are A(3, 0), B(-2, -2), and C(8, 2). 

First, I'll recall that for three points to be collinear, the area of the triangle they form should be zero. Alternatively, the slope between any two pairs of these points should be equal.

I can calculate the slopes between A and B, and then between B and C. If both slopes are the same, the points are likely collinear.

Let me compute the slope between A(3, 0) and B(-2, -2). The formula for slope is (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). So here, it's (-2 - 0)/(-2 - 3) = (-2)/(-5) = 2/5.

Now, the slope between B(-2, -2) and C(8, 2) would be (2 - (-2))/(8 - (-2)) = (4)/(10) = 2/5. 

Since both slopes are equal, this suggests that all three points lie on the same straight line. Therefore, they are collinear.

To determine if the points \( A(3, 0) \), \( B(-2, -2) \), and \( C(8, 2) \) are collinear, we can check if the area of the triangle formed by these points is zero. The formula for the area of a triangle given three points \((x_1, y_1)\), \((x_2, y_2)\), and \((x_3, y_3)\) is:

\[
\text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} |(x_1(y_2 - y_3) + x_2(y_3 - y_1) + x_3(y_1 - y_2))|
\]

Substituting the coordinates of points \( A \), \( B \), and \( C \):

\[
\text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} |3(-2 - 2) + (-2)(2 - 0) + 8(0 - (-2))| \\
= \frac{1}{2} |-12 -4 + 16| \\
= \frac{1}{2} |-0| = 0
\]

Since the area is zero, the points are collinear.

To determine the equation of the line with x-intercept \(a\) and y-intercept \(b\), where the point \((H, K)\) divides the segment between these intercepts in the ratio 1:4 internally, we proceed as follows:

1. **Relate the intercepts to \(H\) and \(K\)**:
   - Since the line is divided in a 1:4 ratio by \((H, K)\), this implies that:
     - \(a = \frac{5H}{4}\)
     - \(b = 5K\)

2. **Formulate the equation of the line**:
   Using the intercept form of a line's equation:
   \[
   \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
   \]
   Substitute \(a\) and \(b\):
   \[
   \frac{x}{\frac{5H}{4}} + \frac{y}{5K} = 1
   \]

3. **Simplify the equation**:
   Multiply numerator and denominator to simplify:
   \[
   \frac{4x}{5H} + \frac{y}{5K} = 1
   \]
   
   To eliminate denominators, multiply both sides by \(20HK\):
   \[
   (4x)(4K) + y(4H) = 20HK
   \]
   Simplifying further:
   \[
   16HKx + 4Hy = 20HK
   \]
   
   Divide through by 4 for simplicity:
   \[
   4HKx + Hy = 5HK
   \]

However, the most straightforward form is the initial simplified equation:

\[
\frac{4x}{H} + \frac{y}{K} = 5
\]

Thus, the final equation of the line is:

\boxed{\dfrac{4x}{H} + \dfrac{y}{K} = 5}

The problem involves determining how many liters of a product can be sold (demanded) at a price of 117 yen based on two given data points. 

We treat these as points on an inverse demand function where quantity demanded increases with price, which might indicate a specific market scenario or exception.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Given Data Points:**
   - At P = 114 yen, Q = 1080 liters.
   - At P = 116 yen, Q = 1320 liters.

2. **Calculate the Slope (m) of the Demand Curve:**
   m = (Q2 - Q1) / (P2 - P1)
   m = (1320 - 1080) / (116 - 114)
   m = 240 / 2
   m = 120 liters per yen.

3. **Determine the Y-Intercept (b):**
   Using one of the points, say (114, 1080):
   Q = mP + b
   1080 = 120 * 114 + b
   1080 = 13680 + b
   b = 1080 - 13680
   b = -12600.

4. **Formulate the Demand Equation:**
   Q = 120P - 12600

5. **Calculate Quantity Demanded at P=117:**
   Q = 120 * 117 - 12600
   Q = 14,040 - 12,600
   Q = 1,440 liters.

**Answer:** He can sell 1,440 liters at a price of 117 yen.

Hmm, I remember that in the unit circle, tan(120°) is negative because it's in the second quadrant where sine is positive and cosine is negative, so their ratio is negative.

Specifically, tan(120°) = tan(180° - 60°) = -tan(60°). And tan(60°) is √3. So tan(120°) should be -√3. Therefore, the slope m is -√3.

Now that I have the slope, I can write the equation of the line using the point-slope form. But since it passes through (0,2), which is the y-intercept, the slope-intercept form would be more straightforward: y = mx + b. Here, b is 2 because when x=0, y=2.

Next, I need to find a line that's parallel to this one but shifted up by 2 units on the y-axis. Since parallel lines have the same slope, the new slope is still -√3. Shifting up by 2 units means adding 2 to the current y-intercept of 2. So the new y-intercept will be 4.

Therefore, the equation of the second line is y = -√3 x + 4.

To determine if there exists a linear function \( f(x) \) with x-intercept \( a \) and y-intercept \( b \) such that the sum of their absolute values is 13, we start by considering the equation of a line in intercept form:

\[ \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1 \]

We need to check if there exist real numbers \( a \) and \( b \) such that \( |a| + |b| = 13 \).

First, we consider the linear function in slope-intercept form:

\[ f(x) = mx + c \]

where \( m \) is the slope and \( c \) is the y-intercept. The x-intercept \( a \) can be found by setting \( f(a) = 0 \):

\[ 0 = ma + c \implies a = -\frac{c}{m} \]

We need to check if there exist real numbers \( m \) and \( c \) such that:

\[ |a| + |b| = \left| -\frac{c}{m} \right| + |c| = 13 \]

This simplifies to:

\[ \left| \frac{c}{m} \right| + |c| = 13 \]

We analyze two cases: \( m > 0 \) and \( m < 0 \).

For both cases, we find that the equation can be satisfied by real numbers \( c \) and \( m \). For example, if we choose \( m = 1 \), then:

\[ |a| + |b| = | -c | + |c| = 2|c| \]

Setting \( 2|c| = 13 \) gives \( |c| = \frac{13}{2} \). Thus, choosing \( c = \frac{13}{2} \) or \( c = -\frac{13}{2} \) will satisfy the condition.

Therefore, we conclude that such linear functions exist where the sum of the absolute values of their intercepts is 13. Hence, the answer is:

\[
\boxed{Yes}
\]

Next, I'll rewrite that as (x + y)/a = 1, which further simplifies to x + y = a. 

Now, since the line passes through the point (4, 5), we can substitute these coordinates into the equation to find 'a'. Plugging in, we get 4 + 5 = a, so a equals 9.

With the value of 'a' known, I can now write the full equation of the line. Substituting back into x/a + y/a = 1 gives us x/9 + y/9 = 1. To make it cleaner, I'll combine the terms to get (x + y)/9 = 1, which simplifies to x + y = 9.

3. **Substitute Point (4,5)**: Plug in x=4 and y=5 into the equation: 4 + 5 = a ⇒ a = 9.

For the x-coordinate: (-2 + 4)/2
That's (2)/2 which equals 1.

For the y-coordinate: (3 + 5)/2
Which is 8/2 = 4.

Hmm, wait, actually distances are usually positive, so maybe there was a typo and they meant the absolute value of the distance is 4 units.

First, I recall that any line can be represented in the slope-intercept form as y = mx + b, where m is the slope and b is the y-intercept. But since the line intersects the x-axis at (5, 0), maybe it's better to use the intercept form of the equation. The intercept form for a line is x/a + y/b = 1, where 'a' is the x-intercept and 'b' is the y-intercept.

Given that the line crosses the x-axis at (5, 0), so a = 5. Plugging this into the intercept form gives me x/5 + y/b = 1. Now, I need to find the value of b. To do this, I can use the formula for the distance from the origin to the line.

First, I need to find the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line defined by the equation 5x - 6y - 28 = 0. To do this, I'll use the formula for calculating the distance from a point to a line in coordinate geometry.

The general formula is:

\[ d = \frac{|Ax + By + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Here, the equation of the line is given as 5x - 6y - 28 = 0. Comparing this with the standard form Ax + By + C = 0, I can identify that A = 5, B = -6, and C = -28.

Next, I'll substitute the coordinates of the point (5, -7) into the formula:

\[ d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}} \]

Calculating the numerator step by step:
\[ 5*5 = 25 \]
\[ -6*(-7) = 42 \]
Adding these along with C:
\[ 25 + 42 - 28 = 39 \]

Now, for the denominator:
\[ 5^2 = 25 \]
\[ (-6)^2 = 36 \]
Adding these gives:
\[ 25 + 36 = 61 \]
Taking the square root of 61.

Putting it all together:
\[ d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} \]

To rationalize the denominator, I can multiply numerator and denominator by √61:

\[ d = \frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61} \]

So, the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x - 6y - 28 = 0 is \( \frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61} \).
</think>

To find the distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line given by the equation \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line in coordinate geometry.

The formula is:
\[
d = \frac{|Ax + By + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]
where \(A\), \(B\), and \(C\) are coefficients from the line equation \(Ax + By + C = 0\).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Coefficients:**
   - The given line equation is \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\).
   - Comparing with the standard form \(Ax + By + C = 0\), we have:
     \[
     A = 5,\quad B = -6,\quad C = -28
     \]
   
2. **Substitute into the Distance Formula:**
   - The point is \((x, y) = (5, -7)\).
   - Plug these values into the formula:
     \[
     d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
     \]
   
3. **Calculate the Numerator:**
   \[
   |5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|
   \]
   
4. **Calculate the Denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]
   
5. **Find the Distance:**
   \[
   d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
   \]
   To rationalize the denominator:
   \[
   d = \frac{39 \cdot \sqrt{61}}{61} = \frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\) is:

{"Final Answer": "39√61⁄61"}
```

The orthocenter of a triangle is the point where all three of its altitudes intersect. In this right-angled triangle, two of the altitudes coincide with the sides of the triangle, and all three meet at the vertex where the right angle is located.

**Step-by-step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Triangle Vertices:** Points A(4,5), B(6,1), and C(3,4).

2. **Determine if Right-Angled:** Calculate side lengths:
   - AB = √[(6-4)² + (1-5)²] ≈ 4.472
   - AC = √[(3-4)² + (4-5)²] ≈ 1.414
   - BC = √[(3-6)² + (4-1)²] ≈ 4.242

3. **Check Pythagorean Theorem:** 
   - AC² + BC² ≈ 2 + 18 = 20, which equals AB².

   Thus, ABC is a right triangle at point C(3,4).

4. **Find Altitudes:**
   - From B to AC: Since it's a right-angled triangle at C, the altitude from B coincides with BC.
   - From A to BC: The altitude falls on BC at point C, so it aligns with side AC.

5. **Orthocenter Location:** All three altitudes intersect at point C(3,4), which is the orthocenter of triangle ABC.

**Answer:**  
The orthocenter is located at \boxed{(3,\ 4)}.

To find the foot of the perpendicular from the point \( P(1, 5) \) to the line \( 3x - 4y = 16 \):

1. **Parametric Method:**
   - The direction vector of the line is \( (3, -4) \).
   - Parametrize the line through \( P \) with parameter \( t \): 
     \[
     x' = 1 + 3t
     \]
     \[
     y' = 5 - 4t
     \]
   - Substitute these into the line equation:
     \[
     3(1 + 3t) - 4(5 - 4t) = 16
     \]
     Simplifying:
     \[
     3 + 9t - 20 + 16t = 16
     \]
     \[
     -17 + 25t = 16
     \]
     Solving for \( t \):
     \[
     25t = 33 \implies t = \frac{33}{25}
     \]
   - Substitute \( t \) back into the parametric equations:
     \[
     x' = 1 + 3 \left( \frac{33}{25} \right) = \frac{124}{25}
     \]
     \[
     y' = 5 - 4 \left( \frac{33}{25} \right) = -\frac{7}{25}
     \]

2. **Verification:**
   - Check if the point satisfies both the line equation and lies on a perpendicular line through \( P \).
   - The constructed line from \( P \) with slope \( -\frac{4}{3} \):
     \[
     y = -\frac{4}{3}x + c
     \]
     Using \( P(1, 5) \):
     \[
     5 = -\frac{4}{3}(1) + c \implies c = \frac{19}{3}
     \]
   - The equation is:
     \[
     y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{19}{3}
     \]
   - Check if \( \left( \frac{124}{25}, -\frac{7}{25} \right) \) lies on this line:
     \[
     y' = -\frac{4}{3} \left( \frac{124}{25} \right) + \frac{19}{3}
     \]
     Simplifying confirms \( y' = -\frac{7}{25} \).

Thus, the foot of the perpendicular from \( P(1, 5) \) to the line \( 3x - 4y = 16 \) is at:

\[
\boxed{\left( \dfrac{124}{25}, -\dfrac{7}{25} \right)}
\]

To determine the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), I first simplify the equation of the line into standard form.

The original equation is:
\[24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\]

Expanding both sides gives:
\[24x + 288 = 10y - 40\]

Rearranging terms to get it in the form \(Ax + By + C = 0\):
\[24x - 10y + 328 = 0\]

Now, applying the distance formula for a point \((x_1, y_1)\) from the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\):
\[d = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}\]

Substituting the values:
\[d = \frac{|24(-2) - 10(2) + 328|}{\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2}}\]
\[d = \frac{|-48 - 20 + 328|}{\sqrt{576 + 100}}\]
\[d = \frac{260}{26.9258}\]

Calculating the final value gives the distance.

$$
\text{Distance} = \frac{|Ax_1 + By_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
$$

$$

One method is the shoelace formula. It works by taking the coordinates in order and applying a specific calculation. The formula is given by:

Area = (1/2) |sum from i=1 to n of (x_i y_{i+1} - x_{i+1} y_i)|

Where the vertices are ordered either clockwise or counterclockwise, and after the last vertex, we loop back to the first one.

- 2*(-3) = -6

- (-2)*7 = -14

- 9*7 = 63

- 0*(-6) = 0

The area of the convex quadrilateral with vertices at (-6,7), (2,9), (7,-3), and (-2,0) can be calculated using the shoelace formula.

Step-by-Step Explanation:
1. List the coordinates in order: A(-6,7), B(2,9), C(7,-3), D(-2,0).
2. Multiply each x-coordinate by the next y-coordinate and sum them up.
   - (-6)*9 + 2*(-3) + 7*0 + (-2)*7 = -54 -6 +0 -14 = -74
3. Multiply each y-coordinate by the next x-coordinate and sum them up.
   - 7*2 + 9*7 + (-3)*(-2) + 0*(-6) = 14 +63+6+0=83
4. Subtract the first sum from the second and take the absolute value: |83 - (-74)| = 157.
5. Divide by 2 to get the area: 157 / 2 = 78.5.

\(m_{AB} = \frac{5 - 4}{3 - 4} = \frac{1}{-1} = -1\)

\(m_{BC} = \frac{-1 - 5}{-1 - 3} = \frac{-6}{-4} = \frac{3}{2}\)

So the slope of BC is \( \frac{3}{2} \).

Lastly, slope of AC: Between A(4,4) and C(-1,-1).

\(m_{AC} = \frac{-1 - 4}{-1 - 4} = \frac{-5}{-5} = 1\)

So the slopes are:
- AB: -1
- BC: \( \frac{3}{2} \)
- AC: 1

Now, to check if any two sides are perpendicular, I need their slopes' product to be -1.

First, let's check AB and AC:

Slope of AB is -1, slope of AC is 1.

Product = (-1) * (1) = -1. Oh, that's -1! So these two lines are perpendicular. That means the triangle has a right angle at point A because sides AB and AC meet there.

BC slope: \( \frac{3}{2} \)

AC slope: 1
BC slope: 3/2
Product = 1*(3/2) = 3/2 ≠ -1. Not perpendicular either.

So only AB and AC are perpendicular, meaning the triangle is right-angled at point A.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

Given points SYS(-4, -5) and HSY(-6, -3), we need to find the angle between the lines formed by these points. 

First, we calculate the slope of the line passing through these points:
- The change in y-coordinates: \((-3) - (-5) = 2\)
- The change in x-coordinates: \((-6) - (-4) = -2\)

Thus, the slope \(m\) is:
\[ m = \frac{2}{-2} = -1 \]

Since both lines are the same (as they pass through the same two points), their slopes are equal. The angle between two parallel lines with equal slopes is 0 degrees.

Therefore, the angle between the two lines is \(\boxed{0}\) degrees.

tan(θ) = |(m2 - m1)/(1 + m1*m2)|

In this case, since one slope is twice the other, substituting m1 as 'm' and m2 as '2m', we get:

tan(θ) = |(2m - m)/(1 + m*(2m))| = |m / (1 + 2m²)|

The problem states that this tan(θ) is equal to 1/3. So, I can set up the equation:

|m / (1 + 2m²)| = 1/3

Bringing all terms to one side:
0 = (1 + 2m²)/3 - m
Multiply through by 3 to eliminate the denominator:
0 = 1 + 2m² - 3m
Rearranging:
2m² - 3m + 1 = 0

This is a quadratic equation in terms of 'm'. Let's solve it using the quadratic formula.

The discriminant D = b² - 4ac = (-3)² - 4*2*1 = 9 - 8 = 1

To find the point \( R \) that divides the line segment between two given points \( P(x_1, y_1) \) and \( Q(x_2, y_2) \) in the ratio 1:4 internally, use the section formula:

\[

\]

b) 1160 = a - 132b

(1080 - 1160) = (a - 114b) - (a - 132b)
-80 = (-114b + 132b)
-80 = 18b
So, b = -80 / 18 ≈ -4.44

Now, substitute b back into equation a:
1080 = a - 114*(-4.44)
a = 1080 + (114 * 4.44) ≈ 1080 + 507.36
a ≈ 1587.36

Now, the demand equation is:
q = a - b * p
= 1587.36 - (-4.44)p
= 1587.36 + 4.44p

To find the quantity sold at p = 117:
q = 1587.36 + (4.44 * 117)
Calculate 4.44 * 117 ≈ 517.08
So, q ≈ 1587.36 + 517.08 ≈ 2104.44 liters

Therefore, the vendor can expect to sell approximately 2104.44 liters per week at a price of 117 per liter.

So, it's both perpendicular to the original line and bisects it, meaning it passes through the midpoint of the segment.

Alright, so my first task is to find the midpoint of the segment connecting (1, 0) and (2, 3). The formula for the midpoint is ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2). Plugging in the values: 

Midpoint M = ((1 + 2)/2, (0 + 3)/2) = (3/2, 3/2). So that's straightforward.

Next, I need to find the slope of the original line segment. The formula for slope is (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁). Substituting the given points:

Slope m = (3 - 0)/(2 - 1) = 3/1 = 3. So the slope of the original line is 3.

Since the perpendicular bisector is perpendicular to this line, its slope will be the negative reciprocal of 3. The negative reciprocal of a number m is -1/m. Therefore:

Slope of perpendicular bisector, let's call it m_perp = -1/3.

Now I have the slope of the perpendicular bisector and a point (the midpoint) through which it passes. So I can use the point-slope form to find its equation. The point-slope formula is y - y₁ = m(x - x₁).

Substituting in the values:

y - 3/2 = (-1/3)(x - 3/2)

Now, let's simplify this equation step by step.

First, distribute the slope on the right side:

y - 3/2 = (-1/3)x + (1/3)*(3/2)

Calculating (1/3)*(3/2): The threes cancel out, leaving 1/2. So,

y - 3/2 = (-1/3)x + 1/2

Now, add 3/2 to both sides to solve for y:

y = (-1/3)x + 1/2 + 3/2

Adding the constants: 1/2 + 3/2 = 4/2 = 2.

So the equation simplifies to:

y = (-1/3)x + 2

Wait, let me double-check that. When I add 1/2 and 3/2, yes, it's indeed 2. So, no mistakes there.

Just to make sure everything is correct, let me verify by plugging the midpoint back into the equation:

y = (-1/3)(3/2) + 2

Calculate (-1/3)*(3/2): The threes cancel out again, leaving -1*(1/2) = -1/2.

So y should be -1/2 + 2 = (which is indeed 3/2). That's correct because the midpoint was (3/2, 3/2), so it fits perfectly into the equation. 

Also, checking if both original points are equidistant from a point on the perpendicular bisector would help confirm. For example, let's take x = 0:

y = (-1/3)(0) + 2 = 2

So one point is (0, 2). Let's compute the distances from this point to both original points.

Distance from (1, 0) to (0, 2):

√[(1 - 0)^2 + (0 - 2)^2] = √[1 + 4] = √5

Distance from (2, 3) to (0, 2):

√[(2 - 0)^2 + (3 - 2)^2] = √[4 + 1] = √5

They are equal, so that's a good sign.

Similarly, taking x = 3/2 (the midpoint's x-coordinate), y = (-1/3)*(3/2) + 2 = (-1/2) + 2 = 3/2, which is the midpoint itself. So distance from both original points should be equal here as well.

Therefore, everything checks out.

Mid_PR_y = (1 + 5) / 2 = 3

Mid_PQ_y = (1 + 3) / 2 = 2
So, the midpoint is (0, 2).

Equation: y = -3x + 7

Equation: y = 3

Using point-slope form: y - 5 = (3/4)(x - 4)

To find the medians of triangle \( PQR \) with vertices \( P(2, 1) \), \( Q(-2, 3) \), and \( R(4, 5) \), we will calculate the midpoints of each side and then determine the equations of the lines connecting these vertices to their opposite midpoints.

### Step 1: Calculate Midpoints

1. **Midpoint of \( QR \):**
   \[
   M_1 = \left( \frac{-2 + 4}{2}, \frac{3 + 5}{2} \right) = (1, 4)
   \]

2. **Midpoint of \( PR \):**
   \[
   M_2 = \left( \frac{2 + 4}{2}, \frac{1 + 5}{2} \right) = (3, 3)
   \]

3. **Midpoint of \( PQ \):**
   \[
   M_3 = \left( \frac{2 - 2}{2}, \frac{1 + 3}{2} \right) = (0, 2)
   \]

### Step 2: Calculate the Equations of the Medians

1. **Median from \( P(2, 1) \) to \( M_1(1, 4) \):**
   - **Slope (\( m \)):**
     \[
     m = \frac{4 - 1}{1 - 2} = \frac{3}{-1} = -3
     \]
   - **Equation:**
     Using the point-slope form \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \):
     \[
     y - 1 = -3(x - 2) \\
     y = -3x + 6 + 1 \\
     y = -3x + 7
     \]

2. **Median from \( Q(-2, 3) \) to \( M_2(3, 3) \):**
   - Since both points have the same y-coordinate:
     The median is a horizontal line.
   - **Equation:**
     \[
     y = 3
     \]

3. **Median from \( R(4, 5) \) to \( M_3(0, 2) \):**
   - **Slope (\( m \)):**
     \[
     m = \frac{2 - 5}{0 - 4} = \frac{-3}{-4} = \frac{3}{4}
     \]
   - **Equation:**
     Using the point-slope form:
     \[
     y - 5 = \frac{3}{4}(x - 4) \\
     y - 5 = \frac{3}{4}x - 3 \\
     y = \frac{3}{4}x + 2
     \]

### Final Answer

The equations of the medians are:

- From \( P(2, 1) \): \( y = -3x + 7 \)
- From \( Q(-2, 3) \): \( y = 3 \)
- From \( R(4, 5) \): \( y = \frac{3}{4}x + 2 \)

The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x -6y -28 =0 is calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line. After plugging in the values and simplifying, the result is:

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the coefficients and constants:**
   - Line equation: 5x -6y -28 = 0
     ⇒ A = 5, B = -6, C = -28
   - Point coordinates: (5, -7)
     ⇒ x₁ = 5, y₁ = -7

2. **Apply the distance formula:**
   Distance = |A*x₁ + B*y₁ + C| / sqrt(A² + B²)

3. **Calculate numerator:**
   = |5*5 + (-6)*(-7) + (-28)|
   = |25 + 42 -28|
   = |39|
   = 39

4. **Calculate denominator:**
   sqrt(5² + (-6)^2)
   = sqrt(25 + 36)
   = sqrt(61)

5. **Compute the distance:**
   Distance = 39 / sqrt(61)

**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': '39/sqrt(61)'}

The first line is 3x - 4y + 12 = 0, so a = 3 and b = -4.

The second line is 3x - 4y + 7 = 0. Here, c1 = 12 and c2 = 7.

Calculating the absolute difference of the constants: |12 - 7| equals 5.

Then, I find the denominator by calculating sqrt(3^2 + (-4)^2) which is sqrt(9 + 16) equal to sqrt(25), so 5.

Finally, dividing 5 by 5 gives a distance of 1.

The equation of the perpendicular bisector for the points (5,6) and (1,4) can be found using the midpoint and slope calculations as explained.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Midpoint:**
   - Using the midpoint formula:
     \[
     \text{Midpoint} = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
     \]
   - Plugging in the points (5,6) and (1,4):
     \[
     \left( \frac{5+1}{2}, \frac{6+4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
     \]

2. **Calculate the Slope of the Original Line:**
   - Using the slope formula:
     \[
     m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
     \]
   - For points (5,6) and (1,4):
     \[
     m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = \frac{1}{2}
     \]

3. **Determine the Perpendicular Slope:**
   - The slope of a perpendicular line is the negative reciprocal of the original slope.
     \[
     m_{\text{perp}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -2
     \]

4. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation:**
   - Using the midpoint (3,5) and the perpendicular slope (-2):
     \[
     y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
     \]
   - Simplify:
     \[
     y = -2x + 6 + 5 \\
     y = -2x + 11
     \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the perpendicular bisector is:
\[
y = -2x + 11
\]

In JSON format, the final answer is:

{"Final Answer": "y = -2x + 11"}
```

To find the distance between the point \((-2, 2)\) and the line defined by \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Simplify the Given Equation
Start by simplifying the equation of the line:
\[
24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)
\]
Divide both sides by \(2\) to simplify calculations:
\[
12(x + 12) = 5(y - 4)
\]
Expand both sides:
\[
12x + 144 = 5y - 20
\]
Rearrange the equation into standard form (\(Ax + By + C = 0\)):
\[
12x - 5y + (144 + 20) = 0 \\
\Rightarrow 12x - 5y + 164 = 0
\]

### Step 2: Use the Distance Formula
The distance \(d\) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is given by:
\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]
Substitute the values from our simplified equation and the point \((-2, 2)\):
\[
A = 12,\quad B = -5,\quad C = 164,\quad x_0 = -2,\quad y_0 = 2
\]
Calculate the numerator:
\[
|12(-2) + (-5)(2) + 164| \\
= |-24 - 10 + 164| \\
= |130|
\]
Calculate the denominator:
\[
\sqrt{12^2 + (-5)^2} = \sqrt{144 + 25} = \sqrt{169} = 13
\]

### Step 3: Compute the Distance
Now, divide the numerator by the denominator to find the distance:
\[
d = \frac{130}{13} = 10
\]

### Final Answer
The distance between the point \((-2, 2)\) and the line \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\) is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: \{ \text{Distance} : 10 \}}
\]

The distance from the point (1, 2) to the line 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 is calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line: 

Distance = |Ax + By + C| / sqrt(A² + B²)
